L2314: ## 19.4.1.3. PPK Review Issues
L2315: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2316: In the Applicant's analysis, an adolescent population PK model adapted from adult model was applied to conduct simulation to evaluate dosage in adolescents due to the lack of PK and clinical data (n=1) in adolescents. This model includes an add-on weight allometry on clearance and volume with fixed exponents (Equation 1) and assessed weight-tiered dosage in adolescents: 40 mg QD in adolescents weighing at least 40 kg, and 20 mg QD in adolescents weighing less than 40 kg (see Figure20).
L2317: Incorporation of weight allometry was deemed reasonable considering an expected physiological difference in drug clearance for hepatic eliminated drugs in adolescents1. This assumption is consistent with reported population PK models of other CYP1A2 metabolized drugs developed inclusive of adolescent data2,3. In those models, at least sex effect is estimated rather than imposed as fixed values because weight is often associated with sex and incorporating both without re-estimation may lead to an underprediction of CL in low body weight females. Therefore, a sensitivity analysis excluding sex effect (see 19.4.1.4) was conducted to further evaluate the proposed dosage, the results of which supported the same recommendation.
L2318: Equation 1. Weight allometry on clearance and volume.
L2319: CL/F \text{ or } Q/F^* (L/h) = 17.8 \times \left( \frac{\text{bodyweight}}{80} \right)^{0.75} \times (1 - 0.337)[\text{if female}]
L2320: V/F^# (L/h) = 371 \times \left( \frac{\text{bodyweight}}{80} \right)^1 \times (1 - 0.331)[\text{if female}]
L2321: Notes: *Q/F refers to Q3/F or Q4/F. #V/F refers to V2/F, V3/F, or V4/F.
L2322: ## Figure20. Boxplots of exposure per weight group and colored by dosage.
L2323: 1 Momper JD, et al, Burckart GJ. Adolescent dosing and labeling since the Food and Drug Administration Amendments Act of 2007. JAMA Pediatr. 2013 Oct;167(10):926-32.
L2324: 2 Lobo ED, et al. Oral olanzapine disposition in adolescents with schizophrenia or bipolar I disorder: a population pharmacokinetic model. Paediatr Drugs. 2010 Jun;12(3):201-11.
L2325: 3 Ma YJ, Jiang DQ, Meng JX, Li MX, Zhao HH, Wang Y, Wang LQ. Theophylline: a review of population pharmacokinetic analyses. J Clin Pharm Ther. 2016 Dec;41(6):594-601. doi: 10.1111/jcpt.12435.

L2332: ## 19.4.1.4. Reviewer's Independent Analysis
L2333: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2334: ## Sensitivity analysis of adolescent extrapolation
L2335: A modified model by removing sex effect was conducted in a sensitivity analysis to address the uncertainty of the combined effect of sex and weight on CL that has not yet been verified by adolescent data. In reviewer's evaluation, virtual patients were curated from available adolescent subjects from pooled NHANES data of 2011-2020. Both Applicant's model and the modified model were included in the analysis and were compared side by side.
L2336: With Applicant's model, exposures of 40 mg QD in adolescents weighing at least 40 kg and 20 mg QD in adolescents weighing less than 40 kg are mainly distributed between the 25th and the 95th percentile of adult exposure, where females are expected to achieve exposure higher than the male counterpart (Figure 21). The results simulated in virtual patients from NHANES are similar to Applicant's results. With the modified model, exposures in female adolescents are reduced but they are still predominantly distributed between the 25th and the 95th percentile of adult exposure (Figure 21).
L2337: Notably, a proportion of adolescents weighing between 40 and 50 kg (~50% in females predicted by Applicant's model) is expected to have exposure levels exceeding the 95th percentile of adult
L2338: exposure with the proposed dosage (40 mg QD), raising potential safety concerns for this subgroup receiving the adult dosage. However, a lower dosage in adolescents of this weight range is deemed unnecessary, considering that 40 kg is commonly used as a threshold for receiving the adult dose, and there remains uncertainty in adolescent PK. With the modified model, only a small proportion is predicted to exceed the 95th percentile of adult exposure, which would be even lower if the exponent of weight effect on CL is less than 0.75. Additionally, the E-R relationship for safety is generally flat for doses up to 300 mg QD (refer to 19.4.2.4), therefore the high exposures in this weight range (the median exposure &lt; 2 folds of that in adults) are not considered to be clinically meaningful.
L2339: In conclusion, the proposed adolescent dosages are acceptable.
L2340: **Figure 21. Boxplots of exposure per weight group and colored by dosage in a sensitivity analysis.**
L2341: Source: Reviewer's analysis.
L2342: Note: Virtual subjects (12 to 16 years old) from 2011-2020 NHANES database were included in the analysis. Reference is adults EBEs population. Dashed lines represent 5th, 25th, 75th and 95th percentiles of adult population.
L2343: ## 19.4.2. Exposure-Response Analysis
L2344: ## 19.4.2.1. ER (Efficacy) Executive Summary
L2345: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2346: Lack of positive E-R relationships for efficacy endpoints was observed across steady-state AUC based on the actual average dose till an event, end of the treatment, or study data cutoff with a planned fixed dosage of 40 mg QD (median relative dose intensity over 99%). E-R relationship for efficacy remains inconclusive given the narrow range and a large variability of exposures at 40 mg QD.
L2347: ## 19.4.2.2. ER (Efficacy) Assessment Summary
L2348: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2349: Data from the pivotal Study AG881-C-004, used for the efficacy ER analysis, included subjects with residual or recurrent Grade 2 glioma with an IDH1 or IDH2 mutation (characteristics of subjects included in the efficacy ER analysis are provided in Table 63) with efficacy endpoints PFS, OR (CR+PR+mR), and CR+PR assessed by BIRC as well as TTNI. The summary of response for each efficacy endpoint is provided in Table 64. The results of the analysis showed that there were no statistically significant relationships between exposure (AUCeot ranged from 343 to 12,800 hr\cdotng/mL) and the 4 evaluated efficacy endpoints (Table 65, Table 66, Table 67, and Table 68), indicating that the ER relationships were flat (defined as no exposure-efficacy relationship observed) at 40 mg QD (Figure 22, Figure 23, Figure 24, and Figure 25). The results for the CR+PR efficacy endpoint are limited due to a low number of responders in this category (n=2).

L2377: General Information
Goal of ER (efficacy) analysis	1. Evaluate the relationships between the steady-state exposure (AUC_{eot}) of vorasidenib, and the efficacy endpoints in subjects with residual or recurrent Grade 2 glioma with an IDH1 or IDH2 mutation treated with vorasidenib in Study AG881-C-004.
2. Determine the effects of disease-related factors and predefined demographic factors on the efficacy endpoints.
Study Included	The ER efficacy analysis evaluated data from subjects with glioma obtained from Study AG881-C-004.
Endpoint	PFS per BIRC
TTNI
OR (CR+PR+mR) per BIRC
CR+PR per BIRC

No. of Patients (total, and with individual PK)	n=167 subjects in the efficacy ER analysis
Population Characteristics	General	Efficacy ER Analysis (N=167)
Age median 41.0 years (21.0, 71.0)
Weight median 79.9 kg (48.0, 155)
n=101 (60.5%) male
n=124 (74.3%) White; n=5 (3.0%) Asian; n=2 (1.2%) Black/African American;
n=36 (21.6%) Other
Pediatrics	Not applicable.
Dose(s) Included	40 mg QD Formulation F2 (N=167)
5 subjects started the study with 50 mg QD Formulation F1, then switched to 40 mg QD Formulation F2 during treatment

Exposure Metrics Explored (range)	Because of strong correlation between AUC, C_{max}, and C_{min}, only AUC was evaluated as exposure metric in the ER efficacy analysis.
The ER efficacy analysis used the area under the curve (AUC_{eot}) at steady state based on the actual average dose across the treatment duration until EOT or up to the study data cutoff.
AUC_{eot} ranged from 343 to 12,800 hr\cdotng/mL.

Covariates Evaluated	Demographic factors: age, weight, sex, race
Disease status: KPS for adults
Concomitant marijuana use was evaluated as 2-category covariate (use / no use) with threshold set at 25% of days on marijuana over the entire subject treatment duration

Final Model Parameters	Summary	Acceptability [FDA's comments]
Model Structure	For PFS and TTNI, a Cox PH model was developed:h(t) = h_0(t) \times \exp\{f(\text{exposure}, \beta_{\text{exposure}}) + \beta_{v_1}(v_1) + \dots + \beta_{v_n}(v_n)\}h_0(t) is a non-parametric baseline hazard. The general function, f, represents the effect of exposure as a predictor of disease progression and \beta_{\text{exposure}}, the coefficient for the effect of the predictor. v_1, \dots, v_n are potential or known risk factors (covariates), and the coefficients (\beta_{v_1}, \dots, \beta_{v_n}) are the corresponding log hazard-ratios.
Yes

L2382: group, the posterior median percentage reduction (95% credible interval) in tumor 2-HG was 64% (22%, 88%) to 93% (76%, 98%) in tumors from patients who received vorasidenib at exposures that were 0.3 to 0.8 times the exposure observed with the recommended dosage."
L2383: ## 19.4.2.3. ER (Safety) Executive Summary
L2384: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2385: Lack of positive trend between exposure and increased risk was identified for any of the evaluated safety endpoints including data from doses 10 mg QD to 300 mg QD. The FDA's independent analysis for ALT/AST/BILI change from baseline further suggests the proposed dose does not present an increased risk of hepatic lab abnormality in the intended population and the rare incidence of hepatic dysfunction could be managed by frequent lab monitoring.
L2386: ## 19.4.2.4. ER (Safety) Assessment Summary
L2387: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L2388: In subjects from Studies AG881-C-002, AG120-881-C-001 and AG881-C-004 (the overview of the studies included in the ER analysis, the subjects' characteristics, and the incidence of each safety endpoint are provided in Table 69, Table 70, and Table 71, respectively), no statistically significant relationships were identified between exposure (AUC_{avg} and C_{max,avg}) and an increased risk of events for the evaluated safety endpoints (ie, rash [all grades and Grade \ge 3], gastrointestinal symptoms [Grade \ge 2], liver dysfunction [ALT or AST \ge 3 \times ULN, and associated with an increase in bilirubin \ge 2 \times ULN [\pm 10 days]], hepatic enzyme elevation (newly occurring or worsening laboratory abnormalities all grades and Grade \ge 2 for ALT, AST, and bilirubin), and neutropenia/leukopenia (all grades and Grade \ge 3). As an example, exposure (AUC_{avg}) ranged between 263 to 12,800 hr\cdotng/mL for the most frequent AE (any grade ALT). For all-grade
L2389: fatigue, a statistically significant relationship was observed, and the risk of fatigue being reported as an AE decreases as the exposure of vorasidenib increases (Table 72, Figure 26). Results were similar when AUCavg or Cmax,avg were used as exposure metric.

L2410: General Information
Goal of ER (safety) analysis	1. Evaluate the relationships between the steady-state exposure (AUC_{avg} and C_{max,avg}) of vorasidenib, and relevant TEAEs in subjects with glioma treated with vorasidenib in Studies AG881-C-002, AG120-881-C-001, and AG881-C-004.
2. Determine the effects of disease-related factors and predefined demographic factors on the safety endpoints.
Study Included	The ER safety analysis evaluated data from subjects with glioma obtained from Studies AG881-C-002, AG120-881-C-001, and AG881-C-004.
Population Included	Subjects with glioma who received at least one dose of vorasidenib and for whom the popPK model was able to provide subject-level PK parameter estimates. Subjects that started on placebo and crossed over to vorasidenib treatment were included in the exposure-safety analysis with baseline conditions set to the start of the vorasidenib treatment.
Endpoint	Rash (all grades and Grade \ge 3)
GI symptoms (Grade \ge 2)
Liver dysfunction (ALT or AST \ge 3 \times ULN, and associated with an increase in bilirubin \ge 2 \times ULN [\pm 10 days])
Hepatic enzyme elevation (newly occurring or worsening laboratory abnormalities all grades and Grade \ge 2 for ALT, AST, and bilirubin)
Fatigue (all grades)
Neutropenia/leukopenia (all grades and Grade \ge 3)

No. of Subjects (total, and with individual PK)	n=255 subjects in the safety ER analysis

Exposure Metrics Explored (range)	
The exposure-safety analysis used AUC_{avg} and C_{max,avg} at steady state based on the actual average dose up to the time of an event, up to the EOT or up to the study data cutoff in case a subject did not experience a TEAE. Per definition, the range of exposure metrics differs for each endpoint. As an example, AUC_{avg} ranged between 263 to 12,800 hr\cdotng/mL and C_{max,avg} ranged between 16.3 and 1,010 ng/mL for the most frequent AE (newly occurring or worsening laboratory abnormalities all grades for ALT)

Covariates Evaluated	Demographic factors: age, weight, sex, race
Concomitant marijuana use was evaluated as 2-category covariate (use / no use) with threshold set at 25% of days on marijuana over the entire subject treatment duration

Final Model Parameters	Summary	Acceptability [FDA's comments]
Model Structure	\logit(P_{i,r,response}) = \log\left(\frac{P_{i,r,response}}{1 - P_{i,r,response}}\right) = \beta_0 + \beta X_i
\beta_0 and \beta are scalar and vector parameters that represent the baseline logit and the effect of predictors, X_i (eg, exposure or baseline covariates) on the logit, respectively.

L2416: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2417: FDA agrees with the E-R analysis conducted by the Applicant.
L2418: ## 19.4.2.5. ER Review Issues
L2419: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2420: Considering the high incidence rate of any Grade ALT and AST elevation (59% and 45%, respectively), the reviewer conducted additional analysis for ALT/AST/BILI change from baseline which is considered a more sensitive measure than lab abnormality AE event (refer to 19.4.2.6).
L2421: ## 19.4.2.6. Reviewer's Independent Analysis
L2422: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2423: To further evaluate the lab abnormalities by lab change from baseline, the ALT/AST/BILI lab was curated from lb datasets of the integrated summary of safety. The lab change over time was first explored graphically. In Study AG881-C-002 that studied a wide dose range, there was a trend of increase for ALT and AST starting from 100 mg QD dose (Figure 27, Figure 28, Figure 29). In pivotal study AG881-C-004 that evaluated 40 mg QD, the changes of ALT, AST, and BILI were minimal and remained stable over time (Figure 30).
L2424: The maximum change of lab (MCHG) for ALT, AST, or BILI was evaluated in an exploratory E-R analysis. There was a step-up trend of increase in ALT/AST/BILI over exposure range in patients
L2425: treated up to 300 mg QD (Figure 31). Six outliers had a remarkable ALT and AST elevation compared to baseline (&gt;500 U/L and &gt;300 U/L, respectively) whose baseline lab values were normal. The exposures of these outliers were within 5th-95th percentile exposure range of 40 mg QD dose except one. Removal of the outliers resulted in a flat trend across exposures for the evaluated lab data.
L2426: In summary, the proposed dosage does not present an increased risk of hepatic lab abnormality in the intended population and the rare incidence of hepatic dysfunction could be managed by frequent lab monitoring.

L2442: ## 19.4.2.7. Overall Benefit-risk Evaluation Based on ER Analyses
L2443: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2444: Exposure did not affect efficacy responses in the studied exposure range. Flat ER profiles at 40 mg QD were observed for PFS, TTNI, OR (CR+PR+mR), and CR+PR, supporting that the efficacy benefit was consistent across the range of exposures achieved in Study AG881-C-004.
L2445: No statistically significant relationship between exposure and increased risk was identified for any of the evaluated safety endpoints. In particular, no statistically significant effect of exposure was identified on ALT, AST, and bilirubin (all grades and Grade \ge 2), indicating that there is no relationship between vorasidenib exposure and elevations in hepatic enzymes of all grades and Grade \ge 2.
L2446: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2447: FDA generally agrees that Applicant's position with regard to exposure response relationship for safety. E-R relationship for efficacy remains inconclusive given the narrow range and a large
L2448: ## 19.4.3. Physiologically Based Pharmacokinetic Modeling
L2449: ## Executive Summary
L2450: The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant's following PBPK report to support the intended uses: "*Development of a PBPK model for vorasidenib with the Simcyp population-based simulator and subsequent evaluation of DDI liability*".
L2451: The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK report and supporting modeling files and reached the following conclusions:
L2452: - The vorasidenib PBPK model was able to capture the vorasidenib plasma Cmax and AUC following a single or multiple oral dose administration of 50 mg vorasidenib or after a single intravenous administration of 0.1 mg vorasidenib in both healthy subjects and cancer patients for formulation 1 and 2 (F1 and F2).
- The vorasidenib PBPK model is adequate to predict the effect of fluvoxamine (a strong CYP1A2 inhibitor), on vorasidenib PK following multiple dose administration of vorasidenib (40 mg QD, F2) in healthy subjects. About 10-fold increase in vorasidenib exposure was predicted when coadministered with fluvoxamine at steady state.
- The vorasidenib PBPK model is adequate to predict the effect of rifampin and phenytoin (moderate CYP1A2 inducers), on vorasidenib PK following multiple dose administration of vorasidenib (40 mg QD, F2) in healthy subjects. About 40% decrease in vorasidenib exposure was predicted when coadministered with rifampin or phenytoin at steady state.
- Due to the uncertainties regarding the in vitro to in vivo extrapolation of the induction parameter values, the vorasidenib model was not considered adequate to accurately predict the effect of vorasidenib on the PK of midazolam (a sensitive CYP3A4 substrate) and repaglinide (a CYP2C8 and CYP3A4 substrate). However, the DDI potential of vorasidenib as a precipitant with CYP3A4 or CYP2C8 substrate cannot be excluded.
- The vorasidenib PBPK model cannot be used to evaluate the potential effect of vorasidenib on the PK of bupropion (a CYP2B6 substrate), because the bupropion substrate models have not been adequately validated.
- The vorasidenib PBPK model is not able to accurately predict the effect of vorasidenib on rosuvastatin (a BCRP substrate) or digoxin (a P-gp substrate) PK following multiple dose administration of vorasidenib (40 mg QD, F2) in healthy subjects. However, the modeling analysis indicated that the exposure changes of rosuvastatin or digoxin when coadministered with vorasidenib are unlikely clinically significant.
L2453: ## Applicant's PBPK Modeling Effort
## PBPK software
L2454: Simcyp V21 (Simcyp Ltd, UK) was used by the Applicant to develop the PBPK models and predict the effects of ciprofloxacin, fluvoxamine, phenytoin, and rifampicin on the PK of vorasidenib and the effects of vorasidenib on the PK of bupropion, repaglinide, warfarin, mephenytoin, midazolam, rosuvastatin and digoxin in healthy subjects.
L2455: ## **Model development**
L2456: The vorasidenib model consisted of an advanced dissolution, absorption and metabolism (ADAM) absorption model and a full PBPK model. P_{\text{eff,man}} (permeability in man) of 6.26 \times 10^{-4} cm/s was predicted based on the permeability data in Caco-2 cells. The intrinsic solubility was 0.0007 mg/mL. The particle sizes were D10 of 3 mm, D50 of 9 mm and D90 of 32 mM. The critical supersaturation ratio of 10 and precipitation rate constant of 4 1/h were used as input to describe the dissolution and precipitation of vorasidenib tablets in GI tract. The f_{u,\text{gut}} (unbound fraction in enterocytes) was set to be 1.0 (Table 73).
L2457: The whole-body PBPK model was used to simulate vorasidenib distribution. The volume of distribution (V_{ss}, 19.2 L/kg) was predicted using Method 3 in Simcyp. The fraction unbound in plasma (f_u) is about 2.66% (Table 73).
L2458: Vorasidenib apparent clearance was estimated to be 15.97 L/h after oral administration of 50 mg vorasidenib in healthy subjects (Study AG881-C-007). CYP1A2 is the primary enzyme responsible for the metabolism of vorasidenib. The f_{m\text{CYP}1\text{A}2} value of 0.97 was estimated based on metabolite profiling and identification in mass balance study (Study AG881-C-005). The rest of the metabolism (0.0215) was assigned as the additional human liver microsome clearance. The absolute bioavailability was 34.1% determined based on the PK data following a single oral dose of 50 mg vorasidenib and a single IV dose of 100 mg vorasidenib in healthy subjects (Study AG881-C-005).
L2459: Following oral administration, 84.7% and 4.52% of the administered dose was recovered in feces and urine with the unchanged parent drug accounting for 55.5% and 0% of the dose administered in feces and urine, respectively (Study AG881-C-005).
L2460: The vorasidenib mediated competition inhibition parameter (K_i) values of BCRP (0.61 mM) and P-gp (5 mM) were obtained from in vitro study (Study NP41967). The vorasidenib mediated induction parameter (\text{Indmax} and \text{IndC}50) values of CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9 and CYP2C19 were obtained from in vitro studies (Study NP41964 and NP41902). For CYP3A4 induction, the \text{Indmax} value of 13.55 was calibrated using rifampicin data, while the \text{IndC}50 of 0.164 mM was obtained from in vitro study (Study NP41964) (Table 73).
L2461: ## **Formulation Modeling Development**
L2462: In the development program for vorasidenib, two different formulations, referred to as Formulation 1 (F1, (b)(4) early phase formulation) and Formulation 2 (F2, intended commercial film-coated formulation), were developed. In healthy volunteer (HV) clinical study AG881-C-006, only F1 was used. In the relative bioavailability Clinical Study AG881-C-007, both formulations (F1
L2463: ## Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
L2464: NDA 218784
L2465: VORANIGO (vorasidenib)
L2466: and F2) were used. In clinical studies AG881-C-008 and PKH-95032-009, only F2 was used. In patients, clinical studies AG881-C-002 and AG120-881-C-001 were performed using F1 only, and a switch between F1 and F2 occurred in clinical study AG881-C-004.
L2467: In clinical study AG881-C-007, the PK of a single dose of vorasidenib following a single dose of 50 mg F1 or 50 mg F2 was evaluated and significant differences in the rate and extent of absorption were observed between these two formulations.
L2468: To account for the impact of the two formulations (F1 and F2) used in the development program on vorasidenib PK, the in vivo dissolution rate was predicted mechanistically using a Diffusion Layer Model (DLM) with different dissolution DLM scalars which were optimized in SIVA v4 using in vitro dissolution profiles (Table 73).

L2479: ## Substrate and precipitant drug models
L2480: The default PBPK models of ciprofloxacin, fluvoxamine, phenytoin, rifampicin, midazolam, bupropion, repaglinide, warfarin, mephenytoin, rosuvastatin and digoxin in SimCYP V21 were used without any modification for DDI prediction.
L2481: ## **PBPK model application**
L2482: The developed PBPK model was used to simulate the DDI for vorasidenib in the following scenarios.
L2483: - To predict the effect of ciprofloxacin (a CYP1A2 inhibitor) and fluvoxamine (a strong CYP1A2 inhibitor) on the PK of vorasidenib.
- To predict the effect of rifampin (a moderate CYP1A2 inducer) and phenytoin (a moderate CYP1A2 inducer) on the PK of vorasidenib.
- To predict the effect of vorasidenib on the PK of midazolam (a CYP3A4 substrate), S-mephenytoin (a CYP2C19 substrate), S-Warfarin (a CYP2C9 substrate), and repaglinide (a CYP2C8 and CYP3A4 substrate).
- To predict the effect of vorasidenib on the PK of bupropion (a CYP2B6 substrate).
- To predict the effect of vorasidenib on the PK of rosuvastatin (a BCRP substrate) and digoxin (a P-gp substrate).
L2484: ## Results
L2485: ## 1. Can vorasidenib PBPK model describe vorasidenib PK in healthy subjects and cancer patients?
L2486: Table 74 and Figure 32 showed the Applicant's model development and verification results. In general, the model was able to capture the vorasidenib C_{max} and AUC following a single or
L2487: multiple oral dose administration of 50 mg vorasidenib or after a single intravenous administration of 0.1 mg vorasidenib. These predictions were validated across both healthy subjects and cancer patients for formulations F1 and F2, with the predicted vorasidenib geometric mean AUC and Cmax values within 0.8-1.25-fold of the observed values.
L2488: However, the model tended to underpredict both Cmax and AUC following multiple oral dose administrations of 100 mg, 200 mg, and 300 mg of vorasidenib at C0D3, C1D15 and C2D1, in cancer patients for F1 (Table 74b). This underprediction at higher dose levels for F1 is likely attributable to the drug's low solubility and specific formulation characteristics, and the model was not able to accurately capture the drug absorption dynamics.
L2489: The model is considered acceptable evaluating the DDI potential as a substrate, since no systemic dose or time dependent nonlinear PK characteristics were observed.
L2490: The assessment of DDI potential as a precipitant should focus solely on the evaluation of vorasidenib at a dosage level approximating 50 mg for F2. Due to the lack of model validation at higher dose levels for F2, the model's ability to predict the effects of vorasidenib on enzyme or transporter substrates at higher doses remains uncertain. Reviewer noted that the Applicant proposed dosages of vorasidenib for adults and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older to be 40 mg QD for patients weighing 40 kg or more, and 20 mg QD for patients weighing less than 40 kg.

L2510: b. Vorasidenib Cmax and AUC ratios (predicted/observed) following multiple oral dose administration of vorasidenib (F1) in cancer patients.
L2511: Source: clinical study AG881-C-002
L2512: ## **2. Can vorasidenib PBPK model predict the effect of fluvoxamine (a strong CYP1A2 inhibitor) on the PK of vorasidenib following multiple dose administration of vorasidenib (40 mg QD, F2)?**
L2513: Yes, the vorasidenib model was validated using clinical DDI data between ciprofloxacin (a moderate CYP1A2 inhibitor) and vorasidenib and the model was adequate to estimate the effect of fluvoxamine on the PK of vorasidenib following multiple dose administration of vorasidenib (40 mg QD, F2) in healthy subjects and cancer patients. About 10-fold increase in vorasidenib exposure was predicted when coadministered with fluvoxamine at steady state (Table 75).
L2514: ## **3. Can vorasidenib PBPK model predict the effect of rifampin (a moderate CYP1A2 inducer) and phenytoin (a moderate CYP1A2 inducer) on the PK of vorasidenib following multiple dose administration of vorasidenib (40 mg QD, F2)?**
L2515: Yes, the vorasidenib model was validated using clinical DDI data between ciprofloxacin (a moderate CYP1A2 inhibitor) and vorasidenib and the model was adequate to estimate the effect of rifampin and phenytoin on the PK of vorasidenib following multiple dose administration of vorasidenib (40 mg QD, F2) in healthy subjects and cancer patients. About 40% decrease in vorasidenib exposure was predicted when coadministered with rifampin or phenytoin at steady state (Table 75).
L2516: ## **4. Can vorasidenib PBPK model predict the effect of vorasidenib on the PK of midazolam (a CYP3A4 substrate), S-mephenytoin (a CYP2C19 substrate), S-Warfarin (a CYP2C9 substrate), and repaglinide (a CYP2C8 and CYP3A4 substrate) following multiple dose administration of vorasidenib (40 mg QD, F2)?**
L2517: ## **5. The Applicant used the in vitro obtained induction parameter (Indmax and IndC50) values to assess the effect of multiple doses of vorasidenib (40 mg QD, F2) on the PK of midazolam, S-mephenytoin, S-warfarin, and repaglinide. The prediction results suggested a positive induction effect of vorasidenib on the PK of midazolam and repaglinide (a dual CYP2C8 and CYP3A4 substrate) (Table 75). Due to the uncertainties regarding the in vitro to in vivo extrapolation of the induction parameter values, the vorasidenib model was not considered adequate to accurately predict the effect of vorasidenib on the PK of midazolam and repaglinide. The DDI potential of vorasidenib as a precipitant with CYP3A4 and CYP2C8 substrates cannot be excluded.**
L2518: ## **6. Can vorasidenib PBPK model predict the effect of vorasidenib on the PK of bupropion (a CYP2D6 substrate) following multiple dose administration of vorasidenib (40 mg QD, F2) in healthy subjects and cancer patients?**
L2519: No. The bupropion and its metabolites models have not been adequately validated4; therefore, they cannot be used to evaluate the potential effect of vorasidenib on the PK of CYP2B6 substrates.
L2520: ## **7. Can vorasidenib PBPK model predict the effect of vorasidenib on the PK of rosuvastatin (a BCRP substrate) and digoxin (a P-gp substrate) following multiple dose administration of vorasidenib (40 mg QD, F2) in healthy subjects and cancer patients?**
L2521: Yes, The Applicant used the in vitro obtained transporter inhibition parameter (K_i) values to assess the effect of multiple dose vorasidenib (40 mg QD, F2) on the PK of rosuvastatin and digoxin. In addition, the effect of vorasidenib on the PK of rosuvastatin and digoxin was further evaluated using a sensitivity analysis of 15-fold (conducted by Applicant) and 50-fold (conducted by FDA reviewer) lower vorasidenib K_i values. These modeling efforts indicate that a positive inhibitory effect of vorasidenib on the PK of rosuvastatin and digoxin would not be expected (Table 75).
L2522: **Table 75** Model predicted effects of CYP1A2 modulators on the PK of vorasidenib and effects of vorasidenib on the PK of CYP3A4, CYP2C19, CYP2C9, BCRP or P-gp substrates following multiple dose administration of 40 mg QD vorasidenib F2.

L2543: Figure 5 is a schematic representation of Study AG881-C-007, a randomized 3-sequence, 4-period crossover study involving 36 healthy subjects. The study design is a 3x4 Latin square design, where 36 subjects are divided into 3 groups (A, B, C, n=12 each) and assigned to one of three sequences (A-B-C, B-C-A, C-A-B) across four periods (Period 1, 2, 3, 4).
The study starts with a Screening phase. Period 1 (n=36) begins after a -28 Days washout period. Period 1 consists of three sequences (A, B, C, n=12 each) receiving different treatments (A, B, or C) in sequence. Period 2 (n=36) follows Period 1 after a +21 Days washout period, consisting of the remaining three sequences (B, C, A, n=12 each). Period 3 (n=36) follows Period 2 after a +21 Days washout period, consisting of the remaining three sequences (C, A, B, n=12 each). Period 4 (n=36) follows Period 3 after a +21 Days washout period, consisting of the remaining three sequences (A, B, C, n=12 each). All subjects in Period 4 receive treatment D (n=36) after a +28 Days washout period, followed by a Final Visit (FU).
Schematic representation of Study AG881-C-007. The study is a 3x4 Latin square design. 36 subjects are divided into 3 groups (A, B, C, n=12 each) and assigned to one of three sequences (A-B-C, B-C-A, C-A-B) across four periods. Period 1 (n=36) starts after a -28 Days washout period. Period 2 (n=36) follows Period 1 after a +21 Days washout period. Period 3 (n=36) follows Period 2 after a +21 Days washout period. Period 4 (n=36) follows Period 3 after a +21 Days washout period, followed by a +28 Days washout period before the Final Visit (FU).
